António Guterres, Secretary-General of the United Nations (UN);\nReaffirming the unwavering commitment of the AU to respect the sovereignty, unity and territorial integrity of the DRC and further reaffirming the solidarity of the AU with the people and the Government of the DRC in their legitimate aspirations for peace, stability and socio-economic development;\nand\nActing under Article 7 of its Protocol, the Peace and Security Council:\n1. Expresses grave concern over the prevailing insecurity and deterioration of the humanitarian and socio-economic development due to violence and human rights abuses committed by the armed groups and negative forces in the eastern DRC;\nreiterates its strong condemnation of the human rights abuses committed by the M23, as well as the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF), Forces Démocratiques de Libération du Rwanda (FDLR) and other armed groups;\n2. Expresses concern over the tensions between the DRC and Rwanda;\nin this regard, calls for calm and greater dialogue between the two countries in furtherance of durable peace in the region;\nencourages them to prioritize peaceful means of addressing the challenges between them;\nreaffirms the importance of building confidence and trust in the region;\n3. Reaffirms that the Framework Agreement remains a viable instrument to support the DRC and institutions in the region to achieve peace and stability, and calls for its urgent revitalization;\n\nemphasizes that signatory countries individually and collectively need to redouble their efforts to fully implement the national and regional commitments enshrined in the Framework Agreement;\n4. In this context, requests the AU, in collaboration with EAC, ECCAS, SADC, ICGLR, and the UN to urgently work towards the revitalization of the Framework Agreement and report back to Council, within six weeks;\n5. Demands that all armed groups, particularly the M23, as well as the ADF, and FDLR to immediately cease hostilities and unconditionally withdraw from the eastern DRC;\nand in this respect, urges for the expeditious implementation of the outcomes of the Luanda and Nairobi processes, as well as the Communiqué of the Mini-Summit of Luanda of 23 November 2022;\n6. Endorses the Final Communique of the Mini-Summit of the EAC and ICGLR on Peace and Security in the Eastern Region of the DRC, held on 17 February 2023 in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia;\n7. Emphasizes the need to support the Expanded Joint Verification Mechanism (EJVM);\ncalls for cantonment and disarmament of M23 under the control of the DRC authorities, and the supervision of EAC Regional Force and the Ad Hoc Verification Mechanism, with the collaboration of United the Nations Organization Stabilization Mission in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (MONUSCO);\n8. Underscores the need to engage in rehabilitation and community development plans to address the issue of ex-combatants and voluntary return of the IDPs and refugees, among others;\n9. Welcomes the deployment of the EAC Regional Force to the eastern DRC and pays tribute to the Troop Contributing Countries (TCCs) to the EAC Regional Force for their commitment to the promotion of regional peace and security;\nand decides to support its deployment and operations;\nin this regard, authorizes the Council in collaboration with the Commission and the Governance Structures of the AU Peace Fund, to immediately work out modalities for the utilization of the Fund and report back to the PSC within six weeks;\ncalls upon the United Nations and other partners to support peace-making efforts in the DRC through the provision of financial and technical expertise;\n10. Strongly condemns the recent attacks against MONUSCO, which resulted in casualties amongst peacekeepers and civilians, the obstruction of access to humanitarian assistance to populations in dire need, and expresses its heartfelt condolences to the bereaved families and the respective Governments;\nreiterates its call for cooperation, coordination and complementarity of efforts between MONUSCO, Forces armées de la République Démocratique du Congo (FARDC) and the EAC Regional Force;\n11. Ensure the Republican Guard and the military are not deployed to places of protests, and ensure justice and full accountability\nfor the killing of dozens of civilians\nby Republican Guards in Goma on August 30, 2023.\nEnsure security forces adhere to international standards regarding peaceful assembly, including the African Commission on Human and Peoples’ Rights (ACHPR)\nGuidelines on Freedom of Association and Assembly in Africa\n.\nRepeal criminal defamation laws in line with the 2010 call by the (ACHPR) in its\nresolution 169\n, which “underlin[ed] that criminal defamation constitute[s] a serious interference with freedom of expression and impedes on the role of the media as a watchdog,” and reform the Digital Code, which\ncriminalizes\nthe sharing of “false news.”\n2.\nPrioritize civilian protection in conflict areas\nMore than 100 armed groups are still active across Congo’s eastern provinces of Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, and Tanganyika. Many of their commanders have been implicated in\nwar crimes\n, including massacres, sexual violence, recruiting children, and pillaging.\nThe war against M23 rebels, a group supported by Rwanda, featured prominently in the last two years of Tshisekedi’s first term and continues to impact civilians, more than one million of whom have been displaced by the fighting and remain in a dire humanitarian situation, according to the United Nations and aid agencies. The M23 controls large swathes of territory in North Kivu. Human Rights Watch has repeatedly called on governments and regional organizations to press Rwanda to stop providing arms and other support to the M23, which is responsible for\ncountless war crimes\nover many years. In light of the M23 offensive,\na coalition of abusive militia\ndubbed “Wazalendo” (“patriots” in Swahili) as well as the Democratic Forces for the Liberation of Rwanda (FDLR) – a largely Rwandan Hutu armed group, some of whose leaders took part in the 1994 genocide in Rwanda –\nreceive support\nfrom the government to fight alongside Congolese soldiers. Tshisekedi’s administration also contracted hundreds of private military personnel to train and instruct army units, and the Congolese army acquired new weaponry, including armed drones, raising concerns of a broader regional conflict.\n\nIn Ituri, militia fighters increased attacks on civilians over the last year,\nincluding at camps for displaced people\n. Ugandan troops continue to track down fighters from the Allied Democratic Forces (ADF), a Ugandan-led armed group with\nties\nto the extremist armed group Islamic State (also known as ISIS), in both North Kivu and Ituri provinces as part of a bilateral agreement with Congo.\nAs the East African Community (EAC) force left Congo at the end of 2023, SADC troops started to deploy in December in North Kivu in support of Congolese government troops. Meanwhile, Tshisekedi’s administration requested that the UN peacekeeping force, MONUSCO, leave the country by the end of 2024.\nTshisekedi starts his second term also\nfacing violence\nin the western province of Mai-Ndombe as\nMobondo militia groups\nattack civilians and government forces in the vicinity of Kinshasa, the nation’s capital.\nRecommendations\nImmediately end support for and collaboration with abusive armed groups forming the Wazalendo coalition as well as with the FDLR in the fight against the M23 armed group. Civilian officials or military personnel credibly implicated in supporting such groups should be suspended from their positions, investigated, and appropriately prosecuted.\nAct on the arrest warrants against members of armed groups, including militia leader Guidon Shimiray Mwissa, who is part of the Wazalendo coalition despite being\nwanted by\nCongolese authorities for abuses and\ncrimes against humanity\n.\nImprove and start the Demobilization, Disarmament, Community Recovery and Stabilization Program (PDDRCS)\nwith long-term, holistic support to deter former fighters from returning to the bush.\nAddress hate speech and incitement, and\nhate-motivated acts of violence\nor intimidation. Ensure accountability for all those responsible for ethnic violence, including the involvement of officials, and work with minority communities to develop effective measures to provide better protection and identify and address problems before they result in criminal offenses.\n3.\nReform the justice system and address corruption\n\nIn\nan interview broadcast\non state television last year, Tshisekedi admitted that lack of progress in the justice system was “a downside to [his] record,” adding that “unfortunately, in our case, justice is destroying our nation… and so I think the justice system needs reform.”\nThe justice system in Congo remains highly dysfunctional, plagued by corruption and the interference of political and military actors. Financial and material shortfalls also seriously hinder judicial processes, many of which simply stall as a result. Many alleged human rights violators have not yet been brought to justice while arbitrary arrests and detentions, including of human rights defenders, journalists, and opposition figures, continue.\nWidespread corruption significantly impacts the capacity of the state to deliver on its obligations to provide quality education, health care, social security, and other economic, social, and cultural rights, and undermines the trust in public authorities and the social contract in the country.\nRecommendations\nIntroduce the necessary systemic reforms to rebuild the judicial system for efficient, accessible, fair, and independent justice.\nEnd political interference and corruption in the judiciary at all levels (including within the Constitutional Court, the country’s highest court). This should include ensuring adequate accountability for those alleged to have engaged in attempts to obstruct or interfere with judicial processes, regardless of their official position.\nFacilitate victims’ access to justice and ensure respect for fair trial rights, including by guaranteeing legal aid for accused who are indigent.\nRelease detainees who have not been convicted of any crime or are awaiting trial in preventive detention without charge beyond time limits prescribed by law.\nTake concrete steps to address corruption, including by ensuring clear procedures to detect, prevent, and investigate incidents of corruption, bribery, and embezzlement of public funds and to bring those responsible for corruption-related offenses to justice.\nEnsure legal protections for whistleblowers and support the passage of a freedom of information act.\nRevise the law on the protection of human rights defenders to remove provisions that criminalize their work.\n4.\nPrioritize accountability for serious crimes\nDuring Tshisekedi’s first term in office,\nnational consultations\nwere held on a potential transitional justice process to address past and present serious crimes under international law. However, the president has not committed to concrete measures to address the recurring cycles of violence and impunity that continues in Congo.\nCongolese courts, mostly military, have taken on an increasing number of cases involving\nwar crimes\nand\ncrimes against humanity\n. This should include ensuring adequate accountability for those alleged to have engaged in attempts to obstruct or interfere with judicial processes, regardless of their official position.\nFacilitate victims’ access to justice and ensure respect for fair trial rights, including by guaranteeing legal aid for accused who are indigent.\nRelease detainees who have not been convicted of any crime or are awaiting trial in preventive detention without charge beyond time limits prescribed by law.\nTake concrete steps to address corruption, including by ensuring clear procedures to detect, prevent, and investigate incidents of corruption, bribery, and embezzlement of public funds and to bring those responsible for corruption-related offenses to justice.\nEnsure legal protections for whistleblowers and support the passage of a freedom of information act.\nRevise the law on the protection of human rights defenders to remove provisions that criminalize their work.\n4.\nPrioritize accountability for serious crimes\nDuring Tshisekedi’s first term in office,\nnational consultations\nwere held on a potential transitional justice process to address past and present serious crimes under international law. However, the president has not committed to concrete measures to address the recurring cycles of violence and impunity that continues in Congo.\nCongolese courts, mostly military, have taken on an increasing number of cases involving\nwar crimes\nand\ncrimes against humanity\n. Still, most atrocities committed in Congo\nremain unpunished\nand domestic proceedings continue to reveal flaws in the national judicial system.\n\nAny future transitional justice process should include and prioritize measures to advance criminal accountability for serious crimes.\nRecommendations\nEstablish an internationalized justice mechanism to investigate and prosecute serious international crimes committed in Congo by both Congolese and foreign actors, including those documented in the\nUN Mapping Report\n(covering crimes committed between 1993 and 2003) and more recently.\nEstablish a formal vetting mechanism to identify and provisionally remove from their posts members of the security forces and other executive branch officers who may have been implicated in serious human rights violations, regardless of rank, while their cases are in progress. Such individuals should be fairly investigated and, if warranted, prosecuted in trials that meet international standards. This should be part of a broader Security Sector Reform.\nEstablish a comprehensive reparations program for victims of serious international crimes and their families, in line with international law, to help them rebuild their lives.\n5.\nReform institutions to strengthen democracy\nKey institutions, particularly the Constitutional Court and the electoral commission (CENI), are perceived to be politicized and favoring the authorities.\nSeveral election observation missions noted the lack of confidence in the December 2023 electoral process. “The impartiality of the Constitutional Court is often called into question,”\nsaid\nthe African Union Electoral Observation Mission. It urged the government to “guarantee the independence and impartiality of the institutions involved in the electoral process.” The Carter Center's election observation mission\nstated\nthat “the elections took place in the context of a deep lack of confidence on the part of many citizens that the elections would be carried out fairly” stemming in part from “the conduct of previous elections, the composition of the [electoral commission], and from gaps in transparency, especially regarding the voter registration process.”\nThe Congolese people’s mistrust in their institutions is reflected in the voter turnout that gradually fell in the four general elections the country organized since its independence, reaching a historical low of 43 percent in December. Even though the main opposition candidates rejected the election results, they refused to file a formal appeal with the Constitutional Court, contending that it would not fairly examine it.\nReforms of the electoral body during Tshisekedi’s first term\ndid not go as far as creating the conditions\nfor an impartial and credible process, in part because the change in its structure and composition did not address its “politicization” issue.\nTshisekedi’s administration should take concrete steps to restore the Congolese people’s trust in democratic institutions.\nRecommendations\nSet up an independent commission of inquiry to impartially investigate and fairly sanction electoral mismanagement, fraud, and corruption that may have undermined the December 2023 general elections. The electoral commission should not be policing its own work.\nReform the electoral commission (CENI) to ensure its independence and credibility, including by guaranteeing that its appointed members are free from any political agenda, and ensuring robust checks and balances.\nGuarantee the independence and impartiality of the Constitutional Court, including by ensuring that the appointment of justices is not controlled by the ruling political force.\nYour tax deductible gift can help stop human rights violations and save lives around the world.", "excerpt_sha256": the US also resumed some of its intelligence and surveillance missions after negotiations with the Nigerien military authorities.\nFootnote\n97\nDRC: tensions with Rwanda, withdrawals of MONUSCO and the EAC Regional Force, and the deployment by the SADC\nAccusations and counteraccusations between the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Rwanda continued during the reporting period of this Digest.\nFootnote\n98\nDuring the UN Security Council meeting on 28 September, for example, the DRC representative accused Rwanda of deploying troops in eastern DRC and supporting the rebel group M-23, and urged the Council to ‘act before it is too late (…) by sanctioning Rwanda’s documented aggression against’ the DRC.\nFootnote\n99\nRwandan representative, on the other hand, accused the DRC for its ‘ongoing collaboration with and arming of the genocidal militia known as the Forces démocratiques de libération du Rwanda (FDLR) and indigenous armed groups’.\nFootnote\n100\nFrench and US representatives criticised both Rwanda and the DRC for providing support to the said armed groups, and urged Rwanda to withdraw from the DRC territory.\nFootnote\n101\nIn a Presidential Statement on 16 October\nFootnote\n102\nand in Resolution 2717 adopted on 19 December,\nFootnote\n103\nthe UN Security Council, without naming any state, condemned ‘support by any external party to M23’ and ‘support to certain armed groups such as the Security Council-sanctioned’ FDLR, and demanded the cessation of such support. A report by the UN Group of Experts on the DRC found ‘further evidence (…) of direct interventions and troop reinforcements by’ Rwanda on the DRC territory, while also noting that Rwanda continued to deny supporting M-23 and having troops in the DRC.\n\nFootnote\n104\nIn another development concerning the DRC, during a meeting of the UN General Assembly on 20 September, the DRC President ‘deplored that peacekeeping missions deployed for 25 years (…) have failed to cope with the rebellions and armed conflicts’, and announced that ‘[t]his is why (…) I instructed the Government of the Republic to begin discussions with the U.N. authorities for an accelerated withdrawal of MONUSCO (…) by bringing forward the start of this progressive withdrawal from December 2024 to December 2023’.\nFootnote\n105\nOn 21 November, the DRC and MONUSCO signed a disengagement plan for the withdrawal of the mission.\nFootnote\n106\nUpon these developments, in Resolution 2717 adopted on 19 December, taking note of a letter from the DRC government ‘on the accelerated withdrawal of MONUSCO from the end of 2023’ and acting under Chapter VII of the UN Charter, the UN Security Council ‘decide[d] to initiate the gradual, responsible and sustainable withdrawal of the mission from DRC’.\nFootnote\n107\nIt also decided ‘to extend until 20 December 2024 the mandate of MONUSCO in the DRC, including, on an exceptional basis and without creating a precedent or any prejudice to the basic principles of peacekeeping, its Force Intervention Brigade’.\nFootnote\n108\nTasks of the mission included carrying ‘out targeted offensive operations in the DRC to neutralise armed groups, through an effective Force Intervention Brigade (FIB), (…) either unilaterally or jointly with the Congolese security forces’.\nFootnote\n109\nAs reported in a previous Digest, the East African Community (EAC) had started the deployment of a regional force in the DRC in 2022 in response to the insecurity in the country.\nFootnote\n110\nIn a Communique on 24 November, the EAC Heads of State ‘[n]oted that DRC will not extend the mandate of the EAC Regional Force beyond 8th December’ and directed the Chiefs of Defense Forces from the EAC and the Southern African Development Community (SADC) to meet before 8 December and submit their recommendations on the way forward.\nFootnote\n111\nThis decision, and the subsequent withdrawal of the EAC Regional Force, came amid criticism by the DRC that the Regional Force failed to enforce the withdrawal of the rebel group M-23 from eastern DRC.\nFootnote\n\n112\nMeanwhile, in a statement on 12 October, the EAC denied the ‘allegations and negative propaganda labelled against the EACRF Contingents, which imply collaboration with Armed groups’.\nFootnote\n113\nThe EAC Regional Force was replaced by a different force from the SADC, which, as reported in the previous Digest, had approved the deployment of a SADC Force in the DRC on 8 May 2023.\nFootnote\n114\nA press release on 4 January 2024 by the SADC announced that the SADC ‘Mission in the Democratic Republic of Congo (SAMIDRC) was deployed on 15 December 2023 to support the Government of the DRC to restore peace and security in the eastern DRC, which has witnessed an increase in conflicts and instability caused by the resurgence of armed groups’.\nFootnote\n115\nAs to the legal basis of the mission, the press release also stated that\nThe deployment of the SAMIDRC is in accordance with the principle of collective self-defence and collective action outlined in the SADC Mutual Defence Pact (2003). The Pact emphasises that; “Any armed attack perpetrated against one of the States Parties shall be considered a threat to regional peace and security and shall be met with immediate collective action”.\nFootnote\n116\nIt is also worth noting that an Extraordinary Summit of the SADC Heads of State and Government held on 11 July ‘reiterated its condemnation of the upsurge of conflicts and hostile activities of all insurgent groups, including M23 rebels and ADF terrorist groups operating in the Eastern DRC, with support from foreign aggressors’.\nFootnote\n117\nIn Resolution 2717 adopted on 19 December, the UN Security Council ‘[e]ncourage[d] support to Regional Forces, as appropriate’.\nFootnote\n118\nMeanwhile, a report by the UN Group of Experts on the DRC found that over 1000 Burundian troops, dressed in the uniform of the DRC troops, were covertly deployed in eastern DRC in support of the DRC Armed Forces since early October 2023. The deployment was outside the EAC Regional Force framework. Burundian officials denied the allegations while the DRC officials said they were unaware of any bilateral cooperation between the two armies.\nFootnote\n119\nBurundi: Burundi accuses Rwanda of supporting rebels\nIn a national radio broadcast on 29 December, the Burundian President accused Rwanda of backing a Burundian armed rebel group based in eastern Congo, known as RED-Tabara, behind an attack on a village in Burundi close to the border with the DRC that killed at least 20 people.", "excerpt_sha256": "6c126ca6b345ef78f8667839947e8e622ec98d2538ffdce1aa9ad6946f4196fc", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}]
